# 12. LDAP integration
> Corporate/L3 deep-dive guide for Red Hat OpenShift AI security and compliance.
> Baseline: OpenShift AI Self-Managed 3.5 and OpenShift Container Platform 4.20; validate all examples against your installed minor release and supported configuration matrix.
## Document control
- **Topic:** LDAP integration
- **Audience:** Senior OpenShift administrators, platform engineers, SREs, DevSecOps engineers, security architects, MLOps engineers, and auditors
- **Depth:** Corporate L3 / 10+ years experience
- **Primary objective:** Synchronize enterprise LDAP or Active Directory users and groups into OpenShift authorization flows with predictable mapping and safe bind credentials.
- **Primary resources:** LDAP identity provider, LDAPSyncConfig, groups, bind secrets, CA bundles, filters, and scheduled synchronization
- **Primary threat focus:** over-broad group import, insecure LDAP, bind credential exposure, stale membership, nested-group surprises, and account mapping errors
- **Lab namespace:** `ai-team-a`
- **Generated:** 2026-07-21
## Learning outcomes
- Explain how **LDAP integration** fits into the OpenShift AI shared-responsibility and defense-in-depth model.
- Identify the OpenShift, Kubernetes, OpenShift AI, identity, storage, network, and supply-chain resources that influence ldap integration.
- Build a working lab from an empty project, apply controls, validate effective behavior, and collect evidence.
- Distinguish authentication, authorization, admission, runtime isolation, data protection, and detective controls.
- Troubleshoot failures without weakening the security baseline or granting broad administrative access.
- Create operational runbooks, service-level objectives, exception records, and audit-ready evidence.
- Translate business and compliance requirements into verifiable OpenShift configuration and repeatable tests.
- Review architectural trade-offs for connected, disconnected, multi-cluster, GPU, and multi-tenant environments.
## 1. Executive overview
LDAP integration is not a single switch. It is an engineered control system spanning identity, namespace design, workload admission, network paths, credentials, container images, model artifacts, datasets, observability, and governance.
The practical security goal is: Synchronize enterprise LDAP or Active Directory users and groups into OpenShift authorization flows with predictable mapping and safe bind credentials.
The main abuse cases are over-broad group import, insecure LDAP, bind credential exposure, stale membership, nested-group surprises, and account mapping errors. A corporate design therefore uses preventive, detective, corrective, and recovery controls rather than relying on one product feature.
OpenShift AI builds on OpenShift security boundaries. OpenShift RBAC decides which API actions a subject can perform. SCC admission constrains pod privilege. NetworkPolicy and OVN controls constrain traffic. Secrets and external identity systems provide credentials. Routes, Gateway API, and TLS protect service exposure. RHACS, signing, scanning, audit, and compliance tools add assurance and evidence.
A mature platform treats AI-specific assets as first-class security objects. Models can execute code or embed malicious behavior. Datasets can contain regulated data or poisoned samples. Prompts and completions can contain confidential information. GPU nodes are expensive shared resources and may require special device access. These realities must be reflected in threat modeling and access design.
The baseline pattern in this guide is deny by default, explicitly allow required flows, separate administrative duties, prefer short-lived identity, pin artifacts by digest, verify provenance, encrypt transport, log security-relevant actions, and regularly test that controls still work.
## 2. Architecture and trust boundaries
| Boundary | Security concern | Required evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Enterprise identity boundary | IdP, MFA, LDAP/OIDC groups, lifecycle source of truth, OAuth tokens | approved design, configuration export, validation result, owner, and review date |
| OpenShift API boundary | Kubernetes API, OpenShift API, admission, RBAC, audit policy | approved design, configuration export, validation result, owner, and review date |
| OpenShift AI control-plane boundary | dashboard, operators, controllers, model registry, pipeline controllers, gateway services | approved design, configuration export, validation result, owner, and review date |
| Tenant project boundary | namespaces, role bindings, service accounts, quotas, NetworkPolicy, PVCs, Secrets | approved design, configuration export, validation result, owner, and review date |
| Compute boundary | worker and GPU nodes, container runtime, SCC, SELinux, seccomp, device plugins | approved design, configuration export, validation result, owner, and review date |
| Data boundary | S3-compatible stores, databases, object prefixes, PVCs, backups, encryption keys | approved design, configuration export, validation result, owner, and review date |
| Supply-chain boundary | Git, CI, image registry, model registry, artifact signing, attestations, SBOMs | approved design, configuration export, validation result, owner, and review date |
| Serving boundary | InferenceService, LLMInferenceService, Services, Routes, Gateways, API tokens, clients | approved design, configuration export, validation result, owner, and review date |
| Evidence boundary | audit logs, SIEM, RHACS, compliance scans, tickets, approvals, exception register | approved design, configuration export, validation result, owner, and review date |
### Reference data flow
```text
User/Automation -> Enterprise IdP -> OpenShift OAuth/API -> OpenShift AI dashboard/controllers
                                      |
                                      +-> Project RBAC -> Workbench / Pipeline / Model Server
                                                            |
                                                            +-> S3 / Registry / Model Registry
                                                            +-> Logs / Metrics / Audit / SIEM
```
## 3. Threat model
1. A user obtains more API permission than their job requires.
2. A workload is admitted with root, privileged, host namespace, or dangerous capability access.
3. A service account token is mounted where no Kubernetes API access is needed.
4. An attacker reads a connection Secret and reuses credentials outside the cluster.
5. A notebook or pipeline exfiltrates data through unrestricted egress.
6. A mutable image tag is replaced after approval.
7. A model file is swapped, poisoned, or loaded through an unsafe serialization format.
8. A dataset contains hidden PII, poisoned samples, or content without an approved purpose.
9. An inference endpoint is published without authentication, authorization, rate control, or trusted TLS.
10. Audit evidence is incomplete, overly sensitive, mutable, or retained for the wrong period.
11. Disconnected mirrors contain stale or unverified content.
12. Emergency access, exceptions, and temporary bindings are never removed.
## 4. Prerequisites
- [ ] A non-production OpenShift cluster with a supported OpenShift AI installation.
- [ ] Cluster-admin access for platform setup and a separate test user for negative authorization tests.
- [ ] The `oc`, `jq`, `yq`, `curl`, `openssl`, `podman`, and Git clients.
- [ ] Optional tools appropriate to the topic: `cosign`, `syft`, `roxctl`, AWS CLI, S3 client, or privacy scanner.
- [ ] A controlled DNS zone and certificate process for route and endpoint tests.
- [ ] An S3-compatible test bucket with versioning and server-side encryption enabled.
- [ ] A private image registry and a repository that supports digest pulls.
- [ ] A security evidence location that test users cannot modify.
- [ ] Documented rollback and break-glass process before enforcing admission or network-deny controls.
## 5. Project from scratch
### Project goal
Build and validate a controlled OpenShift AI tenant demonstrating **LDAP integration** with a repeatable deployment, negative tests, evidence capture, troubleshooting path, and rollback.
### Phase 1 — Define scope and ownership
Name the business owner, technical owner, data owner, security approver, operator, and auditor. Define what is in scope and explicitly list exclusions.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 2 — Create the tenant project
Create `ai-team-a`; apply ownership, environment, data-classification, and cost-center labels; configure quota and limit ranges.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 3 — Establish identity and RBAC
Create or synchronize groups; bind only required roles; avoid direct user bindings except time-bound break-glass access.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 4 — Create purpose-specific service accounts
Use separate identities for workbench, pipeline, model deployment, inference clients, and evidence jobs. Disable automatic token mounting when unnecessary.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 5 — Apply workload security
Use supported default SCCs whenever possible. Set non-root security contexts, drop capabilities, use seccomp, and avoid host access.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 6 — Apply network isolation
Start with default-deny ingress and egress. Add DNS and explicit dependencies. Validate required flows and forbidden cross-tenant flows.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 7 — Configure protected storage and connections
Create least-privilege S3 access, trusted TLS, versioning, encryption, access logging, and a connection Secret with restricted readers.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 8 — Build trusted images and artifacts
Use approved base images, pinned digests, locked dependencies, SBOM generation, vulnerability gates, signatures, and provenance attestations.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 9 — Deploy an AI workload
Create a workbench, pipeline, or model service using the protected identities, storage, image, and network configuration.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 10 — Add endpoint security
Use approved TLS, authentication, authorization, and controlled route or gateway exposure. Separate internal and external endpoints.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 11 — Add monitoring and audit evidence
Capture API events, workload events, network denials, image findings, access logs, and configuration snapshots in protected storage.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 12 — Run positive and negative tests
Prove allowed actions work and denied actions fail. Include unauthorized secret read, cross-tenant network, unsigned image, and expired credential tests where applicable.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 13 — Exercise incident response
Simulate a credential leak or policy violation. Revoke access, isolate workloads, preserve evidence, rotate credentials, and validate recovery.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
### Phase 14 — Review and hand over
Complete an access review, capture residual risk, define SLIs/SLOs, document support boundaries, and obtain owner acceptance.
**Required evidence**
- Configuration export with resource versions and timestamps.
- Command output showing expected allow and deny behavior.
- Named owner and reviewer approval.
- Rollback instruction tested in the lab.
## 6. Core implementation commands
Replace placeholders, test in a non-production namespace, and use supported APIs for your installed release.
```bash
oc get oauth cluster -o yaml
oc adm groups sync --sync-config=ldap-sync.yaml --confirm
oc get groups
oc get group team-a-data-scientists -o yaml
openssl s_client -connect ldap.example.com:636 -showcerts </dev/null
```
## 7. Example manifest or policy skeleton
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: security-control-evidence
  namespace: ai-team-a
data:
  control-owner: platform-security
  review-frequency: quarterly
  evidence-location: s3://security-evidence/openshift-ai/
  exception-process: security-risk-register
```
> Do not apply example security resources blindly. Custom SCCs, cluster-wide OAuth changes, audit profiles, EgressFirewall rules, and admission enforcement can disrupt production workloads.
## 8. Validation strategy
| Test | Expected result | Failure interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| Configuration test | Export the effective resource, not only the Git source. Controllers may mutate or default fields. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
| Authorization test | Use `oc auth can-i` with the exact user or service account and namespace. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
| Admission test | Create a deliberately non-compliant pod and confirm admission rejects it for the expected reason. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
| Network test | Test from the real workload namespace and service account context; verify both allowed and denied destinations. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
| Credential test | Prove the credential can perform required S3/API actions but cannot list, delete, or access unrelated resources. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
| TLS test | Validate hostname, expiry, trust chain, protocol, redirect behavior, and client-side verification without `-k`. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
| Supply-chain test | Resolve the image or model digest, verify signature and attestation, and compare it to the deployed digest. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
| Audit test | Perform a known action and retrieve the corresponding event with subject, verb, resource, namespace, and outcome. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
| Recovery test | Revoke or rotate a credential and demonstrate controlled recovery without unauthorized fallback. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
| Regression test | Automate tests so upgrades and policy changes do not silently weaken or break the control. | investigate policy, identity, controller mutation, trust, or dependency assumptions |
## 9. Troubleshooting decision tree
- Start with the exact failing user, service account, namespace, resource, verb, endpoint, and timestamp.
- Confirm cluster and OpenShift AI component health before changing security controls.
- Check events and status conditions; do not infer the cause from a UI message alone.
- For authorization errors, inspect RoleBindings, ClusterRoleBindings, aggregated roles, and `oc auth can-i`.
- For admission errors, identify the SCC annotation, pod security context, service account, and rejected field.
- For network errors, separate DNS, routing, policy denial, proxy, firewall, TLS, and application readiness.
- For TLS errors, inspect SNI, hostname, chain, CA trust, expiry, key pairing, route termination, and backend certificate.
- For storage errors, test the endpoint and credential independently, then verify connection Secret keys and model path annotations.
- For image errors, resolve the digest, registry auth, mirror rules, signature, scan result, architecture, and pull policy.
- For model errors, verify file format, size, checksum, runtime compatibility, permissions, memory, and readiness probes.
- For audit gaps, validate time synchronization, audit profile, forwarding pipeline, parser, index retention, and query permissions.
- Only after root cause is proven should you change a policy; use the smallest reversible change and document the exception.
## 10. Production hardening baseline
- [ ] Use group-based access and central identity lifecycle management.
- [ ] Separate cluster administration, OpenShift AI administration, security administration, application ownership, and audit review.
- [ ] Avoid wildcard RBAC verbs and resources; review privilege-escalation paths such as pod creation, role binding, impersonation, and secret access.
- [ ] Use restricted SCC behavior, non-root containers, dropped capabilities, seccomp, SELinux, and no host namespace access.
- [ ] Set `automountServiceAccountToken: false` unless Kubernetes API access is required.
- [ ] Use default-deny NetworkPolicy and explicit egress controls for high-risk workloads.
- [ ] Use trusted TLS and managed certificate renewal for dashboards, workbenches, storage, registries, and model endpoints.
- [ ] Prefer short-lived workload identity; otherwise use narrow, rotated credentials with detectable use.
- [ ] Pin images and critical artifacts by digest; verify signatures and provenance before promotion.
- [ ] Generate and retain SBOMs; scan operating-system and language dependencies; define remediation SLAs and exception expiry.
- [ ] Treat model and dataset integrity as supply-chain controls with hashes, signatures, lineage, approval, and immutable versions.
- [ ] Protect PII through discovery, minimization, masking, purpose limitation, retention, and controlled deletion.
- [ ] Centralize audit and security logs; protect them against modification and excessive sensitive-data capture.
- [ ] Test backup, restore, certificate rotation, secret rotation, policy rollback, and disconnected update procedures.
- [ ] Perform quarterly access reviews and event-driven reviews after role changes, incidents, reorganizations, or major platform changes.
## 11. Operational runbook
| Frequency | Activity | Output |
|---|---|---|
| Daily | Review critical security alerts, failed authentications, denied admissions, unusual egress, endpoint errors, and expiring certificates. | ticket, evidence bundle, findings, owners, due dates, and approvals |
| Weekly | Review new vulnerable images, stale scans, failed rotations, unauthorized route changes, and unresolved access requests. | ticket, evidence bundle, findings, owners, due dates, and approvals |
| Monthly | Patch platform dependencies, rebuild custom images, validate backup restore samples, and review privileged identities. | ticket, evidence bundle, findings, owners, due dates, and approvals |
| Quarterly | Perform access certification, tenant isolation tests, policy regression, secret inventory, and evidence completeness review. | ticket, evidence bundle, findings, owners, due dates, and approvals |
| Annually | Re-run threat modeling, privacy impact review, disaster recovery exercise, cryptographic standard review, and control ownership confirmation. | ticket, evidence bundle, findings, owners, due dates, and approvals |
| Event driven | Review after incidents, major upgrades, new identity providers, new storage endpoints, new model families, or regulatory scope changes. | ticket, evidence bundle, findings, owners, due dates, and approvals |
## 12. Incident response playbook
1. Declare the incident and identify commander, platform lead, security lead, data owner, communications lead, and evidence custodian.
2. Preserve relevant API audit, OAuth, route, workload, object-storage, registry, RHACS, and IdP logs.
3. Contain by revoking tokens, disabling compromised credentials, removing bindings, isolating namespaces, or blocking routes and egress.
4. Determine blast radius across users, service accounts, namespaces, buckets, models, datasets, registries, and clusters.
5. Eradicate the cause: patch image, rebuild artifact, rotate secrets, correct policies, remove persistence, and validate clean provenance.
6. Recover through staged deployment, health checks, negative security tests, and heightened monitoring.
7. Complete root-cause analysis, regulatory/privacy assessment, lessons learned, control update, and evidence retention.
## 13. Compliance and evidence mapping
| Control area | Example evidence |
|---|---|
| Access control | RBAC exports, IdP group membership, approvals, access-review decisions, break-glass records |
| Secure configuration | SCC, security contexts, NetworkPolicy, EgressFirewall, route/Gateway TLS, admission policy |
| Cryptographic protection | certificate inventory, trust-chain test, encryption settings, key ownership, rotation record |
| Vulnerability management | SBOM, scan result, triage ticket, SLA, exception, remediation verification |
| Supply-chain integrity | source commit, builder identity, image/model digest, signature, attestation, promotion approval |
| Data protection and privacy | classification, purpose, minimization, DLP result, access log, retention and deletion evidence |
| Audit and accountability | audit policy, event samples, forwarding health, retention configuration, access to evidence |
| Incident management | exercise record, timeline, containment, root cause, corrective actions, closure approval |
| Business continuity | backup policy, restore evidence, disconnected update test, recovery time and recovery point results |
## 14. Senior/L3 design review questions
- What is the authoritative source of truth for ldap integration and how is drift detected?
- Which controls are cluster-wide, namespace-scoped, workload-scoped, data-store-scoped, or external to OpenShift?
- What privilege-escalation paths remain even when normal RBAC appears minimal?
- How are GPU device access, shared memory, high-performance networking, and model caches handled without privileged containers?
- What happens when identity, DNS, certificate, object storage, registry, or SIEM dependencies are unavailable?
- How is the design changed for disconnected operation and how is trust established offline?
- How are model and dataset provenance verified before promotion?
- What security signals indicate credential misuse, model theft, data exfiltration, or tenant crossover?
- Which exceptions exist, who accepted them, when do they expire, and how are compensating controls tested?
- How is control effectiveness measured rather than merely configuration presence?
- How does the design support forensic attribution while minimizing sensitive data in logs?
- How will the security posture be revalidated after an OpenShift or OpenShift AI upgrade?
## 15. Common anti-patterns
- Granting `cluster-admin` to solve a namespace-level workflow problem.
- Binding powerful roles directly to individuals instead of managed groups.
- Creating a custom privileged SCC before proving supported restricted SCC behavior is insufficient.
- Mounting service account tokens and all connection Secrets into every notebook or pipeline step.
- Using one S3 credential for all teams, environments, models, and datasets.
- Allowing unrestricted egress because dependency endpoints were not inventoried.
- Using mutable `latest` tags and treating a successful image pull as provenance.
- Accepting TLS with `curl -k` or disabled S3 certificate validation in production.
- Scanning only at build time and ignoring newly disclosed vulnerabilities.
- Treating model files as inert data and loading unsafe serialization formats from untrusted locations.
- Logging prompts, tokens, credentials, or PII without minimization and retention controls.
- Producing compliance screenshots without machine-readable configuration and repeatable validation.
- Keeping temporary exceptions, emergency access, and old credentials indefinitely.
## 16. Extended L3 control catalog
This catalog turns **LDAP integration** into testable engineering requirements across assets, lifecycle stages, and control families. Use it as a design-review and audit worksheet; remove controls that are demonstrably out of scope rather than ignoring them.
### Control 0001 — Identity and authorization: platform administrator identities during architecture and design
- **Requirement:** For ldap integration, verify subject identity, group ownership, scoped permissions, and separation of duties for platform administrator identities during architecture and design; document scope, owner, approved implementation, and any inherited control.
- **Verification:** Test the effective configuration and a negative case; capture timestamped evidence that identifies the subject, namespace or system, resource, action, and result.
- **Failure mode and response:** Assume over-broad group import, insecure LDAP, bind credential exposure, stale membership, nested-group surprises, and account mapping errors; detect the condition, contain exposure, preserve evidence, correct the narrowest root cause, and re-run regression tests before closure.
### Control 0002 — Workload runtime: platform administrator identities during architecture and design
- **Requirement:** For ldap integration, enforce non-root execution, approved SCC use, resource boundaries, and immutable workload assumptions for platform administrator identities during architecture and design; document scope, owner, approved implementation, and any inherited control.
- **Verification:** Test the effective configuration and a negative case; capture timestamped evidence that identifies the subject, namespace or system, resource, action, and result.
- **Failure mode and response:** Assume over-broad group import, insecure LDAP, bind credential exposure, stale membership, nested-group surprises, and account mapping errors; detect the condition, contain exposure, preserve evidence, correct the narrowest root cause, and re-run regression tests before closure.

- **Verification:** Test the effective configuration and a negative case; capture timestamped evidence that identifies the subject, namespace or system, resource, action, and result.
- **Failure mode and response:** Assume over-broad group import, insecure LDAP, bind credential exposure, stale membership, nested-group surprises, and account mapping errors; detect the condition, contain exposure, preserve evidence, correct the narrowest root cause, and re-run regression tests before closure.

## 17. Final readiness checklist
- [ ] Architecture and data-flow diagrams are current and approved.
- [ ] All human and machine identities have named owners and lifecycle dates.
- [ ] RBAC, SCC, NetworkPolicy, egress, TLS, secret, image, model, dataset, audit, and compliance controls are tested.
- [ ] Positive business workflows and negative abuse cases both pass.
- [ ] Exceptions are documented, time-bound, approved, and monitored.
- [ ] Operational dashboards, alerts, runbooks, and escalation paths exist.
- [ ] Backup, restore, rotation, revocation, incident response, and rollback have been exercised.
- [ ] Evidence is protected, searchable, complete, and retained according to policy.
- [ ] The design has been revalidated against the installed OpenShift and OpenShift AI release documentation.

## 19. Revision notes
- This guide intentionally uses placeholders and conservative security defaults.
- Product APIs and supported integration patterns can change between minor releases.
- Validate preview versus generally available features before production use.
- Record local deviations in your architecture decision record and risk register.
